Pushing the Boundaries of Tractable Multiperspective Reasoning: A Deduction Calculus for Standpoint ℰ ​ ℒ + \mathcal{E\!L}+
Lucía Gómez Álvarez and Sebastian Rudolph and Hannes Strass \affiliations Computational Logic Group, TU Dresden, Germany Center for Scalable Data Analytics and Artificial Intelligence (ScaDS.AI) Dresden/Leipzig, Germany \emails {lucia.gomez_alvarez , sebastian.rudolph , hannes.strass}@tu-dresden.de

Abstract
Standpoint ℰ ​ ℒ \mathcal{E\!L} is a multi-modal extension of the popular description logic ℰ ​ ℒ \mathcal{E\!L} that allows for the integrated representation of domain knowledge relative to diverse standpoints or perspectives. Advantageously, its satisfiability problem has recently been shown to be in PTime , making it a promising framework for large-scale knowledge integration. In this paper, we show that we can further push the expressivity of this formalism, arriving at an extended logic, called Standpoint ℰ ​ ℒ + \mathcal{E\!L}+ , which allows for axiom negation, role chain axioms, self-loops, and other features, while maintaining tractability. This is achieved by designing a satisfiability-checking deduction calculus, which at the same time addresses the need for practical algorithms. We demonstrate the feasibility of our calculus by presenting a prototypical Datalog implementation of its deduction rules.



1
Introduction

The Semantic Web enables the exploitation of artefacts of knowledge representation (e.g., ontologies, knowledge bases, etc.) to support increasingly sophisticated automated reasoning tasks over linked data from various sources.

Description logics
(DLs)
[
2017
,
2011
]
are a prominent class of logic-based KR formalisms in this context since they provide the theoretical underpinning of the Web Ontology Language (OWL 2), the main KR standard by the W3C
[
2009
]
.

In particular, the lightweight description logic
\mathcal{E\!L}

[
2005
]
serves as the core of a popular family of DLs which is the formal basis of OWL 2 EL
[
2009
]
, a widely used tractable profile of OWL 2. One major appeal of the
\mathcal{E\!L}
family is that basic reasoning tasks can be performed in polynomial time with respect to the size of the ontology, enabling reasoning-supported creation and maintenance of very large ontologies.
An example is SNOMED CT
[
2006
]
, which is the largest healthcare ontology and has a broad user base including clinicians, patients, and researchers.

Beyond the scalable reasoning, the Semantic Web must provide mechanisms for the combination and integrated exploitation of the many knowledge sources available. Yet, its decentralised nature has led to the proliferation of ontologies of overlapping knowledge, which inevitably reflect different points of view and follow diverging modelling principles. For instance, in the medical domain, some sources may use the concept
Tumour
to denote a process and others to denote a piece of tissue. Similarly,
Allergy
may denote an allergic reaction or just an allergic disposition. These issues pose well-known challenges in the area of knowledge integration.

Common ontology management approaches fully merge knowledge perspectives, which often requires logical weakening in order to maintain consistency. For instance, an initiative proposed the integration of SNOMED CT with the FMA1140 (Foundational Model of Anatomy) and the NCI (National Cancer Institute Thesaurus), into a unified combination called LargeBio
[
2021
]
, and reported ensuing challenges.
Beyond the risk of causing inconsistencies or unintended consequences, the unifying approach promotes weakly axiomatised ontologies designed to avoid conflict in any context of application at the expense of richer theories that would allow for further inferencing. Hence, while frameworks supporting the integrated representation of multiple perspectives seem preferable to recording the distinct views in a detached way, entirely merging them comes with significant downsides.

This need of handling multiple perspectives in the Semantic Web has led to several logic-based proposals.
The closest regarding goals are multi-viewpoint ontologies
[
2011
,
2018
]
, which often model the intuition of viewpoints in a tailored extension of OWL for which no complexity bounds are given.
Related problems are also addressed in work on contextuality, e.g. C-OWL, DDL, and CKR
[
2003
,
2003
,
2012
]
).

Modal logics are natural frameworks for modelling contexts and perspectives
[
2013
,
2021
]
, and in contrast to tailored multi-perspective frameworks, they benefit from well-understood semantics. However, the interplay between DL constructs and modalities is often not well-behaved and can easily endanger the decidability of reasoning tasks or increase their complexity
[
1995
,
1999
,
1999
]
. Notable examples are
NExpTime
-completeness of the multi-modal description logic
\mathbf{K}_{\mathcal{ALC}}

[
2002
]
and
2
ExpTime
-completeness of
\mathcal{ALC}_{\mathcal{ALC}}

[
2013
]
, a modal contextual logic framework in the style proposed by
?
(
?
).

Standpoint logic

[
2021
]
is a recently proposed formalism that is rooted in modal logic and allows for the simultaneous representation of multiple, potentially contradictory viewpoints and the establishment of alignments between them.
This is achieved by extending a given base logic (propositional logic in the case of
?
, description logic
\mathcal{E\!L}
herein) with labelled modal operators, where
propositions
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{S}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{S}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}$}}}}\phi
and
\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{S}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{S}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}$}}}}\phi
express information relative to the
standpoint

\mathsf{S}
and read, respectively: “according to
\mathsf{S}
, it is
unequivocal/conceivable
that
\phi
”.
Semantically, standpoints are represented by sets of
precisifications
,
1
1

1

Precisifications are analogous to the
worlds
of modal-logic frameworks with possible-worlds semantics.
such that
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{S}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{S}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}$}}}}\phi
and
\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{S}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{S}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}$}}}}\phi
hold if
\phi
is true in
all/some
of the precisifications associated with
\mathsf{S}
.

The following example illustrates the use of standpoint logic for knowledge integration in the medical domain.

Example 1
(Tumour Disambiguation).

A hospital
\mathsf{H}
and a laboratory
\mathsf{L}
have developed their own knowledge bases and aim to make them interoperable.
Hospital
\mathsf{H}
gathers clinical data about patients, which may be used to determine whether a person has priority at the emergency service.
According to
\mathsf{H}
, a
Tumour
is a process by which abnormal or damaged cells grow and multiply (Formula
1
), and patients that conceivably have a
Tumour
have a
HighRisk
priority (Formula
2
).
The laboratory
\mathsf{L}
annotates patients’ radiographs, and models
Tumour
as a lump of tissue (Formula
3
).

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}}}[\mbox{\tt{Tumour}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\mbox{\tt{Process}}]

(1)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}}}[\mbox{\tt{Patient}}\sqcap\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}}}[\exists\mbox{\tt{HasProcess.Tumour}}]

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\mbox{\tt{HighRisk}}]

(2)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{L}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{L}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{L}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{L}$}}}}[\mbox{\tt{Tumour}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\mbox{\tt{Tissue}}]

(3)

Both institutions inherit from
\mathsf{SN}
, which contains the original SNOMED CT as well as patient data (Formula
4
, with the operator
\preceq
encoding the inheritance). Among the background knowledge in
\mathsf{SN}
, we have that
Tissue
and
Process
are disjoint classes (Formula
5
) and that everything that has a part which has a process, has that process itself (Formula
6
).

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}\preceq\mathsf{SN}\qquad\mathsf{L}\preceq\mathsf{SN}\quad

(4)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{SN}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{SN}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{SN}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{SN}$}}}}[\mbox{\tt{Tissue}}\sqcap\mbox{\tt{Process}}\sqsubseteq\bot]\qquad\ \,

(5)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{SN}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{SN}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{SN}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{SN}$}}}}[\mbox{\tt{HasPart}}\circ\mbox{\tt{HasProcess}}\sqsubseteq\mbox{\tt{HasProcess}}]\

(6)

While clearly incompatible due to Formula
5
, the perspectives of
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{L}
are semantically close and and we may be aware of further complex relations between their perspectives. For instance, we might assert that whenever a clinician at
\mathsf{L}
deems that a cancerous lump of tissue is large enough to conceivably be a
Tumour
(tissue), then it is unequivocally undergoing a
Tumour
(process) according to
\mathsf{H}
(Formula
7
).
Or we might want to specify negative information such as non-subsumption between the classes of unequivocal instances of
Process
according to
\mathsf{H}
and to

\mathsf{L}
(Formula
8
).

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{L}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{L}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{L}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{L}$}}}}[\mbox{\tt{Tumour}}]

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}}}[\mbox{\tt{Tissue}}\sqcap\exists\mbox{\tt{HasProcess.Tumour}}]

(7)

\displaystyle\neg(\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}}}[\mbox{\tt{Process}}]

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{L}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{L}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{L}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{L}$}}}}[\mbox{\tt{Process}}])

(8)

Finally, these sources may also have assertional knowledge:

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{SN}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{SN}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{SN}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{SN}$}}}}[\mbox{\tt{Patient}}(p1)\wedge\mbox{\tt{HasPart}}(p1,a)\wedge\mbox{\tt{Colon}}(a)]

(9)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}}}[\mbox{\tt{HasPart}}(a,b)]

(10)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{L}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{L}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{L}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{L}$}}}}[\mbox{\tt{Tumour}}(b)]

(11)

That is, through
\mathsf{SN}
, both
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{L}
know of a patient
p1
and their body parts (Formula
9
) and, in view of some radiograph requested by
\mathsf{H}
on part
b
of this patient’s colon (Formula
10
),
\mathsf{L}
suspects there may be tumour tissue (Formula
11
).

❑

In the first place, one should notice that a naive, standpoint-free integration of the knowledge bases without the standpoint infrastructure would trigger an inconsistency.
Specifically, from a
\mbox{\tt{Tumour}}(b)
instance we could infer both that
\mbox{\tt{Tissue}}(b)
and
\mbox{\tt{Process}}(b)
using the background knowledge of
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{L}
, which in turn would lead to inconsistency with the
\mathsf{SN}
axiom stating
\mbox{\tt{Tissue}}\sqcap\mbox{\tt{Process}}\sqsubseteq\bot
.
Instead, with Standpoint
\mathcal{E\!L}+
, the logical statements (
3
)–(
11
) formalising
Example

1
are not inconsistent, so all axioms can be jointly represented.
On the one hand, we will be able to infer that
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{L}
are indeed incompatible, denoted by
\mathsf{H}\cap\mathsf{L}\preceq\mathbf{0}
and obtained from Formulas (
3
), (
4
), (
5
) and (
10
).
On the other hand, beyond preserving consistency, the use of standpoint logic supports reasoning with and across individual perspectives.

).

Assume that patient
p1
, of which laboratory
\mathsf{L}
detected a tumour tissue (Formula
10
), registers at emergencies in hospital
\mathsf{H}
.
From the knowledge expressed in Formulas (

), we can infer

) and (

)

(12)

​​​​via (

),​ (

) and (

​​​​via (

)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}}}[(\exists\mbox{\tt{HasProcess.Tumour}})(p1)]

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}}}[\mbox{\tt{Patient}}(p1)]

(15)

​​​​via (

),​ (

) and (

)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{H}$}}}}[\mbox{\tt{HighRisk}}(p1)]

(16)

meaning that, according to
\mathsf{H}
,
p1
has a tumour process and is classified as ‘high risk’.

❑

Formally,
Standpoint logics

are multi-modal logics characterised by a simplified Kripke semantics, which brings about beneficial computational properties in different settings. For instance, it is known that adding sentential standpoints (where applying modal operators to formulas with free variables is disallowed)
does not increase the complexity of numerous
NP
-hard
FO-fragments
[
2022
]
, including the expressive DL
\mathcal{SROIQ}b_{s}
, a logical basis of OWL 2 DL
[
2009
]
.

Yet, a fine-grained terminological alignment between different perspectives requires concepts preceded by modal operators, as in Axiom (
7
), which falls out of the sentential fragment.
Recently,
?
(
?
) introduced a
standpoint version
of the description logic
\mathcal{E\!L}
, called Standpoint
\mathcal{E\!L}
, and established that it exhibits
\mathcal{E\!L}
’s favourable
PTime
standard reasoning, while showing that introducing additional features like empty standpoints, rigid roles, and nominals makes standard reasoning tasks intractable.
In this paper, we show that we can push the expressivity of Standpoint
\mathcal{E\!L}
further while retaining tractability.
We present an extended logic, called Standpoint
\mathcal{E\!L}+
, which allows for axiom negation, role chain axioms, self-loops, and other features.
This result is achieved by designing the first satisfiability-checking deduction calculus for a standpoint-enhanced DL, thus at the same time addressing the need for practical algorithms.

Our paper is structured as follows.
After introducing the syntax and semantics of Standpoint
\mathcal{E\!L}+
(denoted
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
) and a suitable normal form (
Section

2
), we establish our main result: satisfiability checking and statement entailment in
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
is tractable.
We show this by providing a particular Hilbert-style deduction calculus (
Section

3
) that operates on axioms of a fixed shape and bounded size, which immediately warrants that saturation can be performed in
PTime
. For said calculus, we establish soundness and refutation-completeness.
In
Section

4
, we briefly describe a proof-of-concept implementation of our approach, showing portions of the actual code to illustrate the key ideas.
We conclude the paper in
Section

5
with a discussion of future work.

An extended version of the paper with proofs of all results is available as a technical appendix.

2
Syntax, Semantics, and Normalisation

We now introduce syntax and semantics of Standpoint
\mathcal{E\!L}+
(referred to as
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
) and propose a normal form that is useful for subsequent algorithmic considerations.

Syntax

A
Standpoint DL vocabulary
is a traditional DL vocabulary consisting of
sets
N_{\mathsf{C}}
of
concept names
,

N_{\mathsf{R}}
of
role names
, and

N_{\mathsf{I}}
of
individual names
, extended by an additional set
N_{\mathsf{S}}
of
standpoint names
with
\hbox{$*$}\in N_{\mathsf{S}}
.
A
standpoint operator
is of the form
\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}
(“diamond”) or
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}
(“box”) with
\mathsf{s}\in N_{\mathsf{S}}
;
we use
\mathord{\mathop{\odot}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}
to refer to either.
2
2

2

We use brackets
[\ldots]
to delimit the scope of the operators.

•

Concept terms
are defined via (where
A\in N_{\mathsf{C}}
,
R\in N_{\mathsf{R}}
)

C\mathrel{::=}\top\mathrel{\hskip-1.0pt\ \mid\ \hskip-1.0pt}\bot\mathrel{\hskip-1.0pt\ \mid\ \hskip-1.0pt}A\mathrel{\hskip-1.0pt\ \mid\ \hskip-1.0pt}C_{1}\sqcap C_{2}\mathrel{\hskip-1.0pt\ \mid\ \hskip-1.0pt}\exists R.C\mathrel{\hskip-1.0pt\ \mid\ \hskip-1.0pt}\mathord{\mathop{\odot}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}C\mathrel{\hskip-1.0pt\ \mid\ \hskip-1.0pt}\exists R.\mathsf{Self}

•

A
general concept inclusion (GCI)
is of the form
C\sqsubseteq D
, where
C
and
D
are concept terms.

•

A
role inclusion axiom (RIA)
is of the form
R_{1}\circ\ldots\circ R_{n}\sqsubseteq R
where
n\geq 1
,
R_{1},\ldots,R_{n},R\in N_{\mathsf{R}}
.

•

A
concept assertion
is of the form
C(a)
, where
C
is a concept term and
a\in N_{\mathsf{I}}
.

•

A
role assertion
is of the form
R(a,b)
, with
a,b\in N_{\mathsf{I}}
and
R\in N_{\mathsf{R}}
.

•

An
axiom

\xi
is a GCI, RIA, or concept/role assertion.

•

A
literal

\lambda
is an axiom
\xi
or a negated axiom
\neg\xi
.

•

A
monomial

\mu
is a conjunction
\lambda_{1}\mathrel{\wedge}\ldots\mathrel{\wedge}\lambda_{m}
of literals.

•

A
formula

\varphi
is of the form
\mathord{\mathop{\odot}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}\mu
for a monomial
\mu
and
\mathsf{s}\in N_{\mathsf{S}}
.

•

A
sharpening statement
is of the form
\mathsf{s}_{1}\cap\ldots\cap\mathsf{s}_{n}\preceq\mathsf{s}
where
n\geq 1
,
\mathsf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{s}_{n}\in N_{\mathsf{S}}
, and
\mathsf{s}\in N_{\mathsf{S}}\cup\left\{\mathbf{0}\right\}
.
3
3

3

\mathbf{0}
is used to express standpoint disjointness as in
\mathsf{s}\cap\mathsf{s^{\prime}}\preceq\mathbf{0}
.

Note that in particular, monomials subsume (finite) knowledge bases of the
\mathcal{E\!L}
family;
monomials even go beyond that in allowing for the occurence of
negated
axioms.
Yet, monomials do not have the full expressive power of arbitrary Boolean combinations of axioms, which is a necessary restriction in order to maintain tractability.

A
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge base
(KB) is a finite set of formulae and possibly negated sharpening statements.
We refer to arbitrary elements of
\mathcal{K}
as
statements
.
Note that all statements except sharpening statements are preceded by modal operators (
“modalised”
for short).

Semantics

The semantics of
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
is defined via standpoint structures.
Given a Standpoint DL vocabulary
\left\langle N_{\mathsf{C}},N_{\mathsf{R}},N_{\mathsf{I}},N_{\mathsf{S}}\right\rangle
, a
description logic standpoint structure
is a tuple

\mathfrak{D}=\mbox{$\left\langle\Delta,\Pi,\sigma,\gamma\right\rangle$}
where:

•

\Delta
is a non-empty set, the
domain
of
\mathfrak{D}
;

•

\Pi
is a set, called the
precisifications
of
\mathfrak{D}
;

•

\sigma
is a function mapping each standpoint symbol to a non-empty
4
4

4

As shown in our prior work
[
2023
]
, allowing for “empty standpoints” immediately incurs intractability, even for an otherwise empty vocabulary.
subset of
\Pi
while we set
\sigma(\mathbf{0})=\emptyset
;

•

\gamma
is a function mapping each precisification from
\Pi
to an “ordinary” DL interpretation
\mathcal{I}=\mbox{$\langle\Delta,\cdot^{\mathcal{I}}\rangle$}
over the domain
\Delta
, where the interpretation function
\cdot^{\mathcal{I}}
maps:

–

each concept name
A\in N_{\mathsf{C}}
to a set
A^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\Delta
,

–

each role name
R{\,\in\,}N_{\mathsf{R}}
to a binary relation
R^{\mathcal{I}}{\,\subseteq\,}\Delta{\times}\Delta
,

–

each individual name
a\in N_{\mathsf{I}}
to an element
a^{\mathcal{I}}\in\Delta
,

and we require
a^{\!\gamma(\pi)}=a^{\!\gamma(\pi^{\prime})}
for all
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\!\in\Pi
and
a\in N_{\mathsf{I}}
.

Note that by this definition, individual names (also referred to as constants) are interpreted rigidly, i.e., each individual name
a
is assigned the same
a^{\gamma(\pi)}\in\Delta
across all precisifications
\pi\in\Pi
.
We will refer to this uniform
a^{\gamma(\pi)}
by
a^{\mathfrak{D}}
.

For each
\pi\in\Pi
, we extend the interpretation mapping
\mathcal{I}=\gamma(\pi)
to concept terms via structural induction:

\displaystyle\top^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}\Delta

\displaystyle\bot^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}\emptyset\

\displaystyle(\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}C)^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}\textstyle\bigcup_{\pi^{\prime}\in\sigma(\mathsf{s})}C^{\gamma(\pi^{\prime})}

\displaystyle(\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}C)^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}\textstyle\bigcap_{\pi^{\prime}\in\sigma(\mathsf{s})}C^{\gamma(\pi^{\prime})}

\displaystyle(C_{1}\sqcap C_{2})^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}C_{1}^{\mathcal{I}}\cap C_{2}^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle(\exists R.C)^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}\left\{\delta\in\Delta\;\middle|\;\mbox{$\left\langle\delta,\varepsilon\right\rangle$}\in R^{\mathcal{I}}\text{ for some }\varepsilon\in C^{\mathcal{I}}\right\}

\displaystyle(\exists R.\mathsf{Self})^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}\left\{\delta\in\Delta\;\middle|\;\mbox{$\left\langle\delta,\delta\right\rangle$}\in R^{\mathcal{I}}\right\}

A role chain expression
\rho=R_{1}\circ R_{2}\circ\ldots\circ R_{n}
is interpreted as
\rho^{\mathcal{I}}\mathrel{\,:=\,}((\cdots(R_{1}^{\mathcal{I}}\circ R_{2}^{\mathcal{I}})\circ\ldots)\circ R_{n}^{\mathcal{I}})
, where, as usual,
R\circ U\mathrel{\,:=\,}\left\{\mbox{$\left\langle x,z\right\rangle$}\;\middle|\;\mbox{$\left\langle x,y\right\rangle$}\in R,\mbox{$\left\langle y,z\right\rangle$}\in U\right\}
.

Satisfaction of a statement
by a DL standpoint structure
\mathfrak{D}
(and precisification
\pi
) is then defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}\hskip-1.0pt,\hskip-1.0pt\pi

\displaystyle\models C\sqsubseteq D

\displaystyle\mathrel{\;:\mathrel{\mkern-6.0mu}{\Longleftrightarrow}\;}

\displaystyle C^{\gamma(\pi)}\subseteq D^{\gamma(\pi)}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}\hskip-1.0pt,\hskip-1.0pt\pi

\displaystyle\models\rho\sqsubseteq R

\displaystyle\mathrel{\;:\mathrel{\mkern-6.0mu}{\Longleftrightarrow}\;}

\displaystyle\rho^{\gamma(\pi)}\subseteq R^{\gamma(\pi)}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}\hskip-1.0pt,\hskip-1.0pt\pi

\displaystyle\models C(a)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\;:\mathrel{\mkern-6.0mu}{\Longleftrightarrow}\;}

\displaystyle a^{\mathfrak{D}}\in C^{\gamma(\pi)}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}\hskip-1.0pt,\hskip-1.0pt\pi

\displaystyle\models R(a,b)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\;:\mathrel{\mkern-6.0mu}{\Longleftrightarrow}\;}

\displaystyle\mbox{$\left\langle a^{\mathfrak{D}},b^{\mathfrak{D}}\right\rangle$}\in R^{\gamma(\pi)}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}\hskip-1.0pt,\hskip-1.0pt\pi

\displaystyle\models\neg\xi

\displaystyle\mathrel{\;:\mathrel{\mkern-6.0mu}{\Longleftrightarrow}\;}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D},\pi\not\models\xi

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}\hskip-1.0pt,\hskip-1.0pt\pi

\displaystyle\models\lambda_{1}\mathord{\,\mathrel{\wedge}}\ldots\mathord{\mathrel{\wedge}\,}\lambda_{n}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\;:\mathrel{\mkern-6.0mu}{\Longleftrightarrow}\;}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D},\pi\models\lambda_{i}\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq n

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}

\displaystyle\models\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}\mu

\displaystyle\mathrel{\;:\mathrel{\mkern-6.0mu}{\Longleftrightarrow}\;}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D},\pi\models\mu\text{ for each }\pi\in\sigma(\mathsf{s})

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}

\displaystyle\models\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}\mu

\displaystyle\mathrel{\;:\mathrel{\mkern-6.0mu}{\Longleftrightarrow}\;}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D},\pi\models\mu\text{ for some }\pi\in\sigma(\mathsf{s})

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}

\displaystyle\models\mathsf{s}_{1}\hskip-1.0pt\mathord{\,\cap}\ldots\mathord{\cap\,}\mathsf{s}_{n}\preceq\mathsf{s}\hskip-8.99994pt

\displaystyle\mathrel{\;:\mathrel{\mkern-6.0mu}{\Longleftrightarrow}\;}

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathsf{s}_{1})\cap\ldots\cap\sigma(\mathsf{s}_{n})\subseteq\sigma(\mathsf{s})

Finally,
\mathfrak{D}
is a
model
of a
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
(written
\mathfrak{D}\models\mathcal{K}
) iff it satisfies every statement in
\mathcal{K}
.
As usual, we call
\mathcal{K}

satisfiable
iff some
\mathfrak{D}
with
\mathfrak{D}\models\mathcal{K}
exists.
A
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
statement
\psi
is
entailed
by
\mathcal{K}
(written
\mathcal{K}\models\psi
) iff
\mathfrak{D}\models\psi
holds for every model
\mathfrak{D}
of
\mathcal{K}
.

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\mu]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\left\{\mathsf{v}\preceq\mathsf{s},\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{v}}}[\mu]\right\}

(17)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\lambda_{1}\mathrel{\wedge}\ldots\mathrel{\wedge}\lambda_{n}]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\left\{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\lambda_{1}],\ \ldots,\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\lambda_{n}]\right\}

(18)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\neg(C\sqsubseteq D)]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A\sqsubseteq C],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A\sqcap D\sqsubseteq\bot],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\top\sqsubseteq\exists R^{\prime}.A]\}

(19)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\neg C(a)]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\left\{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A(a)],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A\sqcap C\sqsubseteq\bot]\right\}

(20)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\neg R(a,b)]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A_{a}(a)],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A_{b}(b)],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A_{a}\sqcap\exists R.A_{b}\sqsubseteq\bot]\}

(21)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\neg(R_{1}\circ\ldots\circ R_{n}\sqsubseteq R)]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\top\sqsubseteq\exists R^{\prime}.A_{a}],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A_{a}\sqcap\exists R.A_{b}\sqsubseteq\bot],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A_{a}\sqsubseteq\exists R_{1}.\cdots\exists R_{n}.A_{b}]\}

(22)

\displaystyle\neg(\mathsf{s}_{1}\mathord{\,\cap}\ldots\mathord{\cap\,}\mathsf{s}_{n}\preceq\mathsf{s})

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\left\{\mathsf{v}\preceq\mathsf{s}_{1},\ldots,\ \mathsf{v}\preceq\mathsf{s}_{n},\ \mathsf{v}\cap\mathsf{s}\preceq\mathbf{0}\right\}\hskip-10.00002pt

(23)

Figure 1:
Normalisation rules for Phase 1.
Therein,
\mathsf{u}\in N_{\mathsf{S}}\cup\left\{\mathbf{0}\right\}
, the
A,A_{a},A_{b}
denote fresh concept names,
R^{\prime}
a fresh role name, and
\mathsf{v}
a fresh standpoint name.

Normalisation

Before we can present our deduction calculus for checking satisfiability of
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge bases, we need to introduce an appropriate normal form.

Definition 1
(Normal Form of
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
Knowledge Bases).

A knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
is in
normal form
iff it only contains statements of the following shapes:

•

sharpening statements of the form
\mathsf{s}\preceq\hbox{$\mathsf{s^{\prime}}$}
and
\mathsf{s}_{1}\cap\mathsf{s}_{2}\preceq\hbox{$\mathsf{s^{\prime}}$}
for
\mathsf{s},\hbox{$\mathsf{s^{\prime}}$},\mathsf{s}_{1},\mathsf{s}_{2}\in N_{\mathsf{S}}
,

•

modalised GCIs of the shape
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[C\sqsubseteq D]
, where

–

C
can be of the form
A
,
\exists R.A
, or
A\sqcap A^{\prime}
with
A,A^{\prime}\in N_{\mathsf{C}}{\,\cup\,}\{\top\}{\,\cup\,}\{\exists R^{\prime}.\mathsf{Self}\mid R^{\prime}\in N_{\mathsf{R}}\}
,
R\in N_{\mathsf{R}}
,

–

D
can be of the form
B
,
\exists R.B
,
\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s^{\prime}}}}B
, or
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s^{\prime}}}}B
with
B{\,\in\,}N_{\mathsf{C}}{\,\cup\,}\{\bot\}{\,\cup\,}\{\exists R^{\prime}.\mathsf{Self}\mid R^{\prime}\in N_{\mathsf{R}}\}
,
R\in N_{\mathsf{R}}
,
\hbox{$\mathsf{s}$},\hbox{$\mathsf{s^{\prime}}$}\in N_{\mathsf{S}}
,
and

–

one of
C,D
is in
N_{\mathsf{C}}\cup\left\{\top\!,\bot\right\}\cup\left\{\exists R.\mathsf{Self}\;\middle|\;R\in N_{\mathsf{R}}\right\}
;

•

modalised RIAs of the form
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[R_{1}\sqsubseteq R_{2}]
and
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[R_{1}\circ R_{2}\sqsubseteq R_{3}]
with
R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}\in N_{\mathsf{R}}
;

•

modalised assertions of the form
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A(a)]
or
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[R(a,b)]
for
a,b\in N_{\mathsf{I}}
,
A\in N_{\mathsf{C}}
, and
R\in N_{\mathsf{R}}
.

❑

Note that complex/nested concepts can only occur on one side of a GCI and then must not nest deeper than one level.

For a given
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
, we can compute its normal form in two phases.
In the first phase, we “break down” formulas into modalised axioms, effectively compiling away negation, and in the second phase we “break down” complex concepts occurring within these axioms.

Phase 1: Modalised Axioms

We obtain the (outer) normal form of axioms by exhaustively applying the transformation rules depicted in
Figure

1
, where “rule application” means that the statement on the left-hand side is replaced with the set of statements on the right-hand side. This eliminates statements preceded by diamonds, modalised axiom sets, and negated axioms.

Phase 2: Restricted Concept Terms

To obtain the (inner) normal forms of concept terms occurring in GCIs as well as the restricted forms of sharpening statements and role inclusion axioms, we use the rules displayed in
Figure

2
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}_{1}\cap\ldots\cap\mathsf{s}_{n}\preceq\mathsf{s}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\left\{\mathsf{s}_{1}\cap\mathsf{s}_{2}\preceq\mathsf{s^{\prime}},\ \mathsf{s^{\prime}}\cap\mathsf{s}_{3}\cap\ldots\cap\mathsf{s}_{n}\preceq\mathsf{s}\right\}

(24)

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}_{1}\cap\ldots\cap\mathsf{s}_{n}\preceq\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{\mathsf{s}_{1}}}}[\top\sqsubseteq A_{1}],\ldots,\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{\mathsf{s}_{n}}}}[\top\sqsubseteq A_{n}],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}[A_{1}\sqcap\ldots\sqcap A_{n}\sqsubseteq\bot]\}

(25)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[R_{1}\circ\ldots\circ R_{n}\sqsubseteq R]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[R_{1}\circ R_{2}\sqsubseteq R^{\prime}],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[R^{\prime}\circ R_{3}\circ\ldots\circ R_{n}\sqsubseteq R]\}

(26)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\bar{C}(a)]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\left\{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A(a)],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A\sqsubseteq\bar{C}]\right\}

(27)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[C\sqsubseteq\top]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\emptyset

(28)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\bot\sqsubseteq D]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\emptyset

(29)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[B\sqsubseteq\exists R.\bar{C}]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\left\{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[B\sqsubseteq\exists R.A],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A\sqsubseteq\bar{C}]\right\}

(30)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[B\sqsubseteq C\sqcap D]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\left\{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[B\sqsubseteq A],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A\sqsubseteq C],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A\sqsubseteq D]\right\}

(31)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[C\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\odot}\nolimits_{\mathsf{u}}}\bar{D}]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\left\{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[C\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\odot}\nolimits_{\mathsf{u}}}A],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A\sqsubseteq\bar{D}]\right\}

(32)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\exists R.\bar{C}\sqsubseteq D]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\left\{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\bar{C}\sqsubseteq A],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\exists R.A\sqsubseteq D]\right\}

(33)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\bar{C}\sqcap D\sqsubseteq E]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\left\{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\bar{C}\sqsubseteq A],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A\sqcap D\sqsubseteq E]\right\}

(34)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{u}}}C\sqsubseteq D]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\left\{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{u}}}[C\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}A],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A\sqsubseteq D]\right\}

(35)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{u}}}C\sqsubseteq D]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad}\{\mathsf{v}_{0}\preceq\mathsf{u},\ \mathsf{v}_{1}\preceq\mathsf{u},\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{u}}}[C\sqsubseteq A],\ \mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{v}_{0}}}A\sqcap\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{v}_{1}}}A\sqsubseteq D]\}

(36)

Figure 2:
Normalisation rules for Phase 2. Therein,
\mathsf{u}\in N_{\mathsf{S}}
,
\bar{C}
and
\bar{D}
stand for complex concept terms not contained in
N_{\mathsf{C}}\cup\left\{\top,\bot\right\}\cup\left\{\exists R.\mathsf{Self}\;\middle|\;R\in N_{\mathsf{R}}\right\}
, whereas each occurrence of
A
(possibly with subscript) on a right-hand side denotes the introduction of a fresh concept name;
each occurrence of
R^{\prime}
on a right-hand side denotes the introduction of a fresh role name;
likewise,
\mathsf{v}
,
\mathsf{v}_{0}
, and
\mathsf{v}_{1}
denote the introduction of a fresh standpoint name.
Rule (
34
) is applied modulo commutativity of
\sqcap
.

The first three transformation rules are novel, the others were already proposed and formally justified in our earlier work
[
2023
]
.

A careful analysis yields that the overall transformation (Phase 1 + Phase 2) has the desired semantic and computational properties.

Lemma 1
.

Any
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
can be transformed into a
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge base
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
in normal form such that:

•

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
is a
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
-conservative extension of
\mathcal{K}
,

•

the size of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
is at most linear in the size of
\mathcal{K}
, and

•

the transformation can be computed in
PTime
.

In particular,
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
being a
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}}
-conservative extension of
\mathcal{K}
means that
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
are equisatisfiable.

Reasoning problems and reductions

The deduction calculus we are going to present in the next section decides the fundamental reasoning task of satisfiability for
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
:

Problem:

\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}\ \textsc{Knowledge base satisfiability}

Input:

\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
.

Output:

yes
, if
\mathcal{K}
has a model,
no
otherwise.

This reasoning task is useful in itself, e.g. for knowledge engineers to check for grave modelling errors that turn the specified knowledge base globally inconsistent. From a user’s perspective, however, a more application-relevant problem is that of statement entailment, as it allows to “query” the specified knowledge for consequences:

Problem:

\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}\ \textsc{Statement entailment}

Input:

\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
,
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
statement
\phi
.

Output:

yes
, if
\mathcal{K}\models\phi
,
no
otherwise.

Typically, entailment
\mathcal{K}\models\phi
can be (many-one-)reduced to unsatisfiability of
\mathcal{K}\cup\left\{\neg\phi\right\}
.
This is not immediately possible in the case of
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
due to its restricted syntax: note that despite the possibility to negate single axioms or sharpening statements, the negation of monomials or formulae in general is not supported by the syntax of
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
. However, it turns out that the a similar technique can be applied with some additional care.

It is clear that the straightforward reduction works for
arbitrary modalised literals
\mathord{\mathop{\odot}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\lambda]
,
for negated formulas
\neg\mathord{\mathop{\odot}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\mu]
,
and for (possibly negated) sharpening statements,
so what remains to be detailed is the reduction for modalised monomials.
Consider
\mathord{\mathop{\odot}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}\mu=\mathord{\mathop{\odot}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\lambda_{1}\mathrel{\wedge}\ldots\mathrel{\wedge}\lambda_{n}]
.
For
\mathord{\mathop{\odot}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}=\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}
, we have that
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}\mu
is logically equivalent to
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\lambda_{1}]\mathrel{\wedge}\ldots\mathrel{\wedge}\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\lambda_{n}]
and thus we can (Turing-)reduce checking entailment
\mathcal{K}\models\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\mu]
to checking whether all of
\mathcal{K}\cup\left\{\neg\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\lambda_{1}]\right\}
, …,
\mathcal{K}\cup\left\{\neg\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\lambda_{n}]\right\}
are unsatisfiable (which is still polynomial in
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\mu]
).
Finally, for
\mathord{\mathop{\odot}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}=\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}
, we employ the idea underlying normalisation rule (
17
) to obtain the following:

Lemma 2
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge base with normal form
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
and
\mu
be a monomial.
It holds that
\mathcal{K}\models\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\mu]
if and only if
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\models\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{u}}}[\mu]
for some
\mathsf{u}\in N_{\mathsf{S}}
with
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\models\mathsf{u}\preceq\mathsf{s}
.

Proof sketch.

The main idea is that for
\mathcal{K}\models\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\mu]
to hold there
is a formula
\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s^{\prime}}}}[\mu^{\prime}]\in\mathcal{K}
with
\mathcal{K}\models\mathsf{s^{\prime}}\preceq\mathsf{s}
and formulas
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{\mathsf{s}_{1}}}}[\mu_{1}],\ldots,\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{\mathsf{s}_{m}}}}[\mu_{m}]\in\mathcal{K}
with
\mathcal{K}\models\mathsf{s^{\prime}}\preceq\mathsf{s}_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
(where neither type of formula is strictly required, and
\mathsf{s^{\prime}}=\mathsf{s}
in case no
\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s^{\prime}}}}
formula is involved), such that
\left\{\mu^{\prime},\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}\right\}\models\mu
.
In case all relevant formulas are of the form
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{\mathsf{s}_{i}}}}[\mu_{i}]
, then
\mathcal{K}\models\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\mu]
and the claim trivially holds (with
\mathsf{u}=\mathsf{s}
).
In case some
\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{\mathsf{s^{\prime}}}}}[\mu^{\prime}]\in\mathcal{K}
is involved, normalisation rule (
17
) will introduce the new standpoint name
\mathsf{u}
that can serve as witness in the normalised KB
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
.

So to decide
\mathcal{K}\models\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\mu]
, we normalise
\mathcal{K}
into
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
and then successively enumerate
\mathsf{s^{\prime}}\in N_{\mathsf{S}}
occurring in
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
for which
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\models\mathsf{s^{\prime}}\preceq\mathsf{s}
and test
\mathcal{K}\models\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s^{\prime}}}}[\mu]
for each.
In view of these considerations, we arrive at the following reducibility result.

Theorem 3
.

There exists a
PTime
Turing reduction from
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}

Statement entailment
to
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}

knowledge base satisfiability
.

Thus, every tractable decision procedure for satisfiability can be leveraged to construct a tractable entailment checker. Therefore, we will concentrate on a method for the former.

3
Refutation-Complete Deduction Calculus for Normalised KBs

In this section, we present the Hilbert-style deduction calculus for
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
.
Premises and consequents of the calculus’ deduction rules will be axioms in normal form with one notable exception:
We allow for extended versions of modalised GCIs of the general shape

\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{t}}}[A\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[B\Rightarrow C]],

(37)

the meaning of which should be intuitively clear, despite the fact that
\Rightarrow
is not a connective available in
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
. In terms of more expressive Standpoint DLs, such an axiom could be written

\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{t}}}[A\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\neg B\sqcup C]]
,
but this would obfuscate the “Horn nature” of the statement. Note that the axiom can be expressed in
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
by the two axioms
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{t}}}[A\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}D]
and
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[D\sqcap B\sqsubseteq C]
using an auxiliary fresh concept
D
. Yet, for better treatment in the calculus, we need all the information “bundled” within one axiom. With this new axiom type in place, we dispense with axioms of the shapes
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A\sqsubseteq B]
and
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s^{\prime}}}}B]
, replacing them by
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}[\top\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A\Rightarrow B]]
and
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[A\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s^{\prime}}}}[\top\Rightarrow B]]
, respectively. Similarly, we will replace concept assertions of the form
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}A(a)
by
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}[\{a\}\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\top\Rightarrow A]]
, where
\{a\}
is understood as a “nominal concept”, to be interpreted by the singleton set
\{a^{\mathfrak{D}}\}
in the usual way.
5
5

5

Despite us using this convenient representation “under the hood”, we emphasise that our calculus is not meant to be used with input knowledge bases with free use of nominals. In fact, we have shown that extending Standpoint
\mathcal{E\!L}
by nominal concepts leads to intractability
[
2023
]
.

Then, it should be clear that these are equivalent axiom replacements.

As one final preprocessing step, we introduce, for every concept
\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}B
that occurs in the normalised KB and every ABox individual
a
, a fresh standpoint symbol denoted
\mathsf{s}[a,B]
and a fresh concept name
P_{\mathsf{s},a,B}
and add the following background axioms:

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}[a,B]

\displaystyle\preceq\mathsf{s}

(38)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}[\{a\}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[B\Rightarrow P_{\mathsf{s},a,B}]]

(39)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}[P_{\mathsf{s},a,B}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}}_{[a,B]}[\top\Rightarrow B]]

(40)

Intuitively, the purpose of this conservative extension is that, whenever
a
is required to satisfy
\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}B
, it will satisfy
B
in all
\mathsf{s}[a,B]
-precisifications, this way arranging for a concrete, “addressable” witness for
\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}B(a)
.

Given an
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
, let
\mathcal{K}^{\mathrm{prep}}
denote its preprocessed variant, obtained through normalisation and the steps described above. Again note that
\mathcal{K}^{\mathrm{prep}}
can be computed in deterministic polynomial time.
Now let
\mathcal{K}^{\vdash}
denote the set of axioms obtained from
\mathcal{K}^{\mathrm{prep}}
by saturating it under the deduction rules of
Figure

3
.
We note that each axiom type has a bounded number of parameters, each of which can be instantiated by a polynomial number of elements (concepts, roles, individuals, standpoints) occurring in
\mathcal{K}^{\mathrm{prep}}
. Consequently, the overall number of distinct inferrable axioms is polynomial and therefore the saturation process to obtain
\mathcal{K}^{\vdash}
runs in deterministic polynomial time.
We will see in the next section that these observations give rise to a worst-case optimal Datalog implementation of the saturation procedure.

-inferences

{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}[\top\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}[\top\Rightarrow\bot]]}\end{array}

Figure 3:
Deduction calculus for
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}

We next argue that the presented calculus has the desired properties.
As usual, soundness of the calculus is easy to show and can be argued for each deduction rule separately by referring to the definition of the semantics.
What remains to be shown is a particular type of completeness: Among the inferrable axioms, the particular intrinsically contradictory statement
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}[\top\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}[\top\Rightarrow\bot]]
will play the pivotal role of indicating unsatisfiability of
\mathcal{K}
(also referred to as refutation).
We will show that our calculus is
refutation-complete
, meaning that for any unsatisfiable
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
, we have that
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}[\top\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}[\top\Rightarrow\bot]]\in\mathcal{K}^{\vdash}
.
More concretely, we prove the contrapositive by establishing the existence of a model whenever
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}[\top\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}[\top\Rightarrow\bot]]\not\in\mathcal{K}^{\vdash}
.
This model is canonical in a sense but, as opposed to canonical models of the
\mathcal{E\!L}
family, it will typically be infinite.

Canonical Model Construction

Given a
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
with
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}[\top\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}[\top\Rightarrow\bot]]\not\in\mathcal{K}^{\vdash}
, we construct a model
\mathfrak{D}
of
\mathcal{K}
in an infinite process:
we start from an initialised model
\mathfrak{D}_{0}
and extend it (both by adding domain elements and precisifications) in a stepwise fashion, resulting in a “monotonic” sequence of models.
The result of the process is the “limit” of this sequence, which can be expressed via an infinite union.

For the initialisation, we choose the standpoint structure

\mathfrak{D}_{0}=\mbox{$\left\langle\Delta_{0},\Pi_{0},\sigma_{0},\gamma_{0}\right\rangle$}
where:

•

\Delta_{0}
consists of one element
\delta_{a}
for every individual name
a
mentioned in
\mathcal{K}
;

•

\Pi_{0}
consists of one precisification
\pi_{\mathsf{s}}
for every standpoint
\mathsf{s}
mentioned in
\mathcal{K}
(including
*
);

•

\sigma_{0}
maps each standpoint
\mathsf{s}
to
\{\pi_{\mathsf{s}}\}\cup\{\pi_{\mathsf{s^{\prime}}}\mid\hbox{$\mathsf{s^{\prime}}$}\preceq\hbox{$\mathsf{s}$}\in\text{$\mathcal{K}$}^{\vdash}\}
;

•

\gamma_{0}
maps each
\pi_{\mathsf{s}}
to the description logic interpretation
\mathcal{I}
over
\Delta_{0}
, where

–

a^{\mathcal{I}}=\delta_{a}
for each individual name
a
,

–

A^{\mathcal{I}}=\{\delta_{a}\mid\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{u}}}[\{a\}\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\top{\Rightarrow}A]]{\,\in\,}\text{$\mathcal{K}$}^{\vdash}\!\!,\hbox{$\mathsf{s}$}{\,\in\,}\sigma^{\!-1}_{0}(\pi_{\mathsf{s}})\}
for every concept name
A
, and

–

R^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(\delta_{a},\delta_{b})\mid\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[R(a,b)]\in\text{$\mathcal{K}$}^{\vdash},\hbox{$\mathsf{s}$}\in\sigma^{-1}_{0}(\pi_{\mathsf{s}})\}

for each role name
R
.

It can be shown that the obtained structure satisfies all axioms of
\mathcal{K}
except for those of the shape
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[E\sqsubseteq\exists R.F]
.
This will also be the case for all structures
\mathfrak{D}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{D}_{2}
, … subsequently produced.
The sequence arises by iteratively adding more domain elements in order to satisfy more axioms of the type
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[E\sqsubseteq\exists R.F]
.
Thereby, the concept and role memberships of pre-existing elements with respect to pre-existing standpoints will remain unchanged.
This also justifies the definition of a labelling function
\Lambda_{\pi}
immutably assigning to every domain element
\delta
a set of concepts, satisfied in
\pi
.
For
\mathfrak{D}_{0}
,
we let
\Lambda_{\pi}
map elements
\delta_{a}
according to

\Lambda_{\pi}(\delta_{a})=\{C\mid\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{u}}}[\{a\}\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\top{\Rightarrow}C]]\in\text{$\mathcal{K}$}^{\vdash},\hbox{$\mathsf{s}$}\in\sigma^{-1}_{0}(\pi)\}.

As discussed above, after having arrived at a structure
\mathfrak{D}_{i}=\mbox{$\left\langle\Delta_{i},\Pi_{i},\sigma_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\rangle$}
, we inspect if
\mathfrak{D}_{i}
satisfies all axioms of the form
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{t}}}[E\sqsubseteq\exists R.F]
. If so,
\mathfrak{D}_{i}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
and we are done. Otherwise, we pick some
\delta^{*}\in\Delta_{i}
and some
\pi^{*}\in\Pi_{i}
with
\pi^{*}\in\sigma(\mathsf{t})
and
\delta^{*}\in E^{\gamma(\pi^{*})}\setminus(\exists R.F)^{\gamma(\pi^{*})}
for some (previously unsatisfied) axiom
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{t}}}[E\sqsubseteq\exists R.F]
from
\mathcal{K}
.
Among the eligible pairs
\delta^{*}
,
\pi^{*}
, we pick one for which the value
\min\{j\leq i\mid\delta^{*}\in\Delta_{j}\}+\min\{j\leq i\mid\pi^{*}\in\Pi_{j}\}
is minimal; this ensures fairness in the sense that any axiom violation will ultimately be addressed.
Given
\delta^{*}
and
\pi^{*}
we now obtain
\mathfrak{D}_{i+1}=\mbox{$\left\langle\Delta_{i+1},\Pi_{i+1},\sigma_{i+1},\gamma_{i+1}\right\rangle$}
from

\mathfrak{D}_{i}=\mbox{$\left\langle\Delta_{i},\Pi_{i},\sigma_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\rangle$}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[C\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s^{\prime}}}}[D\Rightarrow E]]

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathtt{gci\_nested(s,C,s^{\prime},D,E)}

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}_{1}\cap\mathsf{s}_{2}\preceq\mathsf{s}_{3}

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathtt{sharper\_intersection(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[C\sqcap D\sqsubseteq E]

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathtt{gci\_conjunction\_left(s,C,D,E)}

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}_{1}\preceq\mathsf{s}_{2}

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathtt{sharper(s_{1},s_{2})}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\exists R.C\sqsubseteq D]

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathtt{gci\_existential\_left(s,R,C,D)}

\displaystyle R_{1}\circ R_{2}\sqsubseteq R_{3}

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathtt{ria3(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[C\sqsubseteq\exists R.D]

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathtt{gci\_existential\_right(s,C,R,D)}

\displaystyle R_{1}\sqsubseteq R_{2}

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathtt{ria2(R_{1},R_{2})}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[C\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s^{\prime}}}}D]

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathtt{gci\_diamond\_right(s,C,s^{\prime},D)}

Figure 4:
Datalog atoms used to represent normal-form statements of
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
as used in the deduction calculus.
Therein,
\mathsf{s}_{1},\mathsf{s}_{2},\mathsf{s}_{3},\mathsf{s},\mathsf{s^{\prime}}\in N_{\mathsf{S}}
,
C,D,E\in N_{\mathsf{C}}
, and
R_{1},R_{2},R_{3},R\in N_{\mathsf{R}}
.

•

\Delta_{i+1}=\Delta_{i}\cup\{\delta^{\prime}\}
, where
\delta^{\prime}
is a fresh domain element;

•

let
\mathrm{Con}(F,\pi^{*})
denote the concepts subsumed by
F
under
\pi^{*}
, i.e.,
\{A\mid\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{u}}}[\top\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[F\Rightarrow A]]\in\text{$\mathcal{K}$}^{\vdash}\!,\hbox{$\mathsf{s}$}\in\sigma^{-1}(\pi^{*})\}

•

\Pi_{i+1}
is obtained from
\Pi_{i}
by adding a fresh precisification
\pi_{\delta^{\prime},\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}D}

whenever there is some
C\in\mathrm{Con}(F,\pi^{*})
with
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{t}}}[C\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}D]\in\text{$\mathcal{K}$}^{\vdash}
for some
\mathsf{t}\in\sigma^{-1}(\pi^{*})

•

let
\sigma_{i+1}
be such that
\sigma_{i+1}(\hbox{$\mathsf{s^{\prime\prime}}$})=\sigma_{i}(\hbox{$\mathsf{s^{\prime\prime}}$})\cup\left\{\pi_{\delta^{\prime},\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}D}\right\}
if
\hbox{$\mathsf{s^{\prime\prime}}$}\in\{\hbox{$\mathsf{s}$}\}\cup\{\hbox{$\mathsf{s^{\prime}}$}\mid\hbox{$\mathsf{s}$}\preceq\hbox{$\mathsf{s^{\prime}}$}\in\text{$\mathcal{K}$}^{\vdash}\}
and
\sigma_{i+1}(\hbox{$\mathsf{s^{\prime\prime}}$})=\sigma_{i}(\hbox{$\mathsf{s^{\prime\prime}}$})
otherwise.

•

for
\pi\in\Pi_{i+1}
we let
\Lambda_{\pi}(\delta^{\prime})
be

\displaystyle\mathrm{Con}(F,\pi^{*})

\displaystyle\text{ if }\pi=\pi^{*},

\displaystyle\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\displaystyle{\bigcup}\\[3.5736pt]
\mathrlap{{}^{\!{G\in\mathrm{Con}(F,\pi^{*})},\ {\mathsf{s}\in\sigma^{-1}_{i+1}(\pi)},\ {\mathsf{t}\in\sigma^{-1}_{i+1}(\pi^{*})}}}\\[-3.91805pt]
\end{array}\{A\mid\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{t}}}[G\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\top\Rightarrow A]]\in\text{$\mathcal{K}$}^{\vdash}\}

\displaystyle\text{ if }\pi\in\Pi_{i}\setminus\{\pi^{*}\},

\displaystyle\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\displaystyle{\bigcup}\\[3.5736pt]
\mathrlap{{}^{\!{G\in\mathrm{Con}(F,\pi^{*})},\ {\mathsf{s}\in\sigma^{-1}_{i+1}(\pi)},\ {\mathsf{t}\in\sigma^{-1}_{i+1}(\pi^{*})}}}\\[-3.91805pt]
\end{array}\{A\mid\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{t}}}[G\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[D\Rightarrow A]]\in\text{$\mathcal{K}$}^{\vdash}\}

\displaystyle\text{ if }\pi=\pi_{\delta^{\prime},\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}D}.

•

Let
\gamma_{i+1}
be the interpretation function defined as follows:

–

a^{\gamma_{i+1}(\pi)}=a^{\gamma_{i}(\pi)}=\delta_{a}
for each individual name
a
and each
\pi\in\Pi_{i+1}
.

–

for concept names
A
, we let
A^{\gamma_{i+1}(\pi)}=A^{\gamma_{i}(\pi)}\cup\{\delta^{\prime}\}
if
A\in\Lambda_{\pi}(\delta^{\prime})
, and
A^{\gamma_{i+1}(\pi)}=A^{\gamma_{i}(\pi)}
otherwise.

–

for all role names
T
, we obtain
T^{\gamma_{i+1}(\pi)}
essentially by performing a concurrent saturation process under all applicable RIAs, that is,

T^{\gamma_{i+1}(\pi)}=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}[T^{\gamma_{i+1}(\pi)}]_{k},

where we let

[T^{\gamma_{i+1}(\pi)}]_{0}=\mathit{Self}\cup\mathit{Other}
, with

\displaystyle\mathit{Self}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\{(\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\}&\text{ if }\exists T.\mathsf{Self}\in\Lambda_{\pi}(\delta^{\prime}),\\
\emptyset&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathit{Other}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\emptyset\qquad\quad\text{ whenever }\pi\in\Pi_{i+1}\setminus\Pi_{i}\\
T^{\gamma_{i}(\pi)}\quad\text{ for }\pi\in\Pi_{i},\text{ if }\ T\neq R\text{ or }\pi\neq\pi^{*}\\
R^{\gamma_{i}(\pi^{*})}\cup(\delta^{*}\!,\delta^{\prime})\quad\ \text{ if }T=R\text{ and }\pi=\pi^{*},\end{cases}

and obtain

\displaystyle[T^{\gamma_{i+1}(\pi)}]_{k+1}\,=\ \

\displaystyle[T^{\gamma_{i+1}(\pi)}]_{k}\cup\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\displaystyle{\bigcup}\\[3.5736pt]
\mathrlap{{}^{\!\hskip-12.05554pt{\mathsf{s}\in\sigma^{-1}_{i+1}(\pi)},\ {\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[R_{0}\sqsubseteq T]\in\mathcal{K}^{\vdash}}}}\\[-3.91805pt]
\end{array}\ [R_{0}^{\gamma_{i+1}(\pi)}]_{k}

\displaystyle\cup\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\displaystyle{\bigcup}\\[3.5736pt]
\mathrlap{{}^{\!\hskip-12.05554pt{\mathsf{s}\in\sigma^{-1}_{i+1}(\pi)},\ {\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[R_{1}\circ R_{2}\sqsubseteq T]\in\mathcal{K}^{\vdash}}}}\\[-3.91805pt]
\end{array}\ [R_{1}^{\gamma_{i+1}(\pi)}]_{k}\circ[R_{2}^{\gamma_{i+1}(\pi)}]_{k}.

After producing the (potentially infinite) sequence
\mathfrak{D}_{0},\mathfrak{D}_{1},\ldots
we obtain the wanted model
\mathfrak{D}
via

\mathfrak{D}=\mbox{$\left\langle\Delta,\Pi,\sigma,\gamma\right\rangle$}=\mbox{$\left\langle\bigcup_{i}\Delta_{i},\bigcup_{i}\Pi_{i},\bigcup_{i}\sigma_{i},\bigcup_{i}\gamma_{i}\right\rangle$}.

We then establish that the
\mathfrak{D}
resulting from this construction indeed is a well-defined structure that satisfies all axioms of
\mathcal{K}
. To this end, an important observation is that for all domain elements
\delta\in\Delta
and precisifications
\pi\in\Pi
of
\mathfrak{D}
, it holds that
C\in\Lambda_{\pi}(\delta)
implies
\delta\in C^{\gamma(\pi)}
. Furthermore, we show that if
\bot\in\Lambda_{\pi}(\delta)
were to hold for any
\delta\in\Delta
and
\pi\in\Pi
(which is the only way the model construction could possibly fail, by declaring an existing domain element to be contradictory), then this would necessarily imply
\mathcal{K}^{\vdash}\models\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}[\top\sqsubseteq\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{*}}}[\top\Rightarrow\bot]]
. By virtue of these considerations, we arrive at the aspired result.

Theorem 4
.

The deduction calculus displayed in
Figure

3
is sound and refutation-complete for
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge bases.

Then, together with our previous insights and
Theorem

3
, we can use
Theorem

4
to establish tractability of the fundamental standard reasoning tasks in
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
.

Corollary 5
.

\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}

knowledge base satisfiability
and
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}

Statement entailment
are
PTime
-complete.

Thereby,
PTime
-hardness follows immediately from the
PTime
-hardness of reasoning in plain
\mathcal{E\!L}
.

4
Datalog-Based Implementation

We have prototypically implemented our approach in the Datalog-based language
Soufflé

[
2016
]
.
The prototype’s source code is available from our group’s github site at
https://github.com/cl-tud/standpoint-el-souffle-reasoner
.
The implementation currently does not scale well, so optimising both calculus and implementation is an important issue for future work.

Figure 5:
Datalog implementation of the calculus excluding rules dealing with assertions and self-loops.
Predicates with prefix “
\mathtt{is\_}
” declare the vocabulary:

C\in N_{\mathsf{C}}\leadsto\mathtt{is\_cn(C)}
,

R\in N_{\mathsf{R}}\leadsto\mathtt{is\_rn(R)}
,

\mathsf{s}\in N_{\mathsf{S}}\leadsto\mathtt{is\_sn(s)}
, and

a\in N_{\mathsf{I}}\leadsto\mathtt{is\_nom(\left\{a\right\})}
.
Note that
deduction rule schema (S.3) needs multiple concrete instantiations.
The symbols
\mathtt{*}
,
\top
, and
\bot
are used for readability here;
in the actual implementation, we use proper (but globally fixed) Datalog constants.

Calculus

The calculus of
Section

3
is implemented in the pure Datalog fragment of
Soufflé
’s input language.
Following the common approach, as e.g. detailed by
?
(
?
), we introduce a predicate symbol for each possible (normal-form) formula shape as shown in
Figure

4
.
Implementing the deduction calculus then boils down to writing Datalog rules for all deduction rules;
the main rules of the calculus are shown in
Figure

5
(p.
5
).
For the axiom schemas (tautologies) we make use of helper predicates that keep track of the vocabulary;
likewise, for nominals and self-loops we use binary predicates to translate back and forth between individual names/nominal concepts, and role names/self-loop concepts, respectively, hence treating nominals and self-loops as “ordinary” concept names.

Normalisation

For obtaining the normal form of a given
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge base in its full expressiveness, we employ several
Soufflé
features that are not strictly Datalog.
For one, we use algebraic data types
6
6

6

https://souffle-lang.github.io/types#algebraic-data-types-adt
to define term-based encodings of all structured constructs involved in representing knowledge bases, such as concept terms, axioms, formulas, etc., where the base types “standpoint name”, “role name”, and “concept name” are subtypes of the built-in type
\mathtt{symbol}
(i.e., string).
More importantly, during normalisation we employ
Soufflé
’s built-in functor
\mathtt{cat}
7
7

7

https://souffle-lang.github.io/arguments#intrinsic-functor
for concatenating strings to create unambiguous identifiers for newly introduced concept, role, and standpoint names.

5
Conclusion and Future Work

In this paper, we presented the knowledge representation formalism Standpoint
\mathcal{E\!L}+
, which extends the formerly proposed Standpoint
\mathcal{E\!L}
language
[
2023
]
by a row of new modelling features: role chain axioms and self-loops, extended sharpening statements including standpoint disjointness, negated axioms, and modalised axiom sets.
We designed a deduction calculus that is sound and refutation-complete when applied to appropriately pre-processed
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge bases.
As preprocessing and exhaustive deduction rule application are shown to run in
PTime
, we thereby established tractability of statisfiability checking of
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge bases and – by virtue of a
PTime
Turing reduction – also tractability of checking the entailment of
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
statements from
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge bases, notably also allowing negated statements.

We note that, if tractability is to be preserved, the options of further extending the expressivity of
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
are limited.
Clearly, any modelling feature that would turn the description logic
\mathcal{E\!L}
intractable – atomic negation, disjunction, cardinality restrictions, universal quantification as well as inverse or functional roles
[
2005
]
– would also destroy tractability of
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
.
But also the free use of nominal concepts, which is known to still warrant
PTime
reasoning when added to
\mathcal{E\!L}
with role chain axioms and self-loops, has been shown to be computationally detrimental as soon as standpoints are involved.
The same holds if one allows for the declaration of roles to be rigid or for a more liberal semantics that would admit empty standpoints
[
2023
]
.

Beyond the theoretical advancement, we also believe that the developed deduction calculus can pave the way to practical reasoner implementations by means of Datalog materialisation, a method already proven to be competitive for reasoning in lightweight description logics. In order to demonstrate the principled feasibility of this approach, we implemented a publicly available prototype in
Soufflé
.

There are numerous avenues for future work. While the calculus is adequate to show our theoretical results and demonstrate feasibility, we are confident that there is much room for improvement when it comes to optimisation.
We expect that refactoring the set of deduction rules can significantly improve performance in practice.
In Datalog terms, it would be beneficial to reduce the number and arity of the predicates involved, the number of variables per rule, and the number of alternative derivations of the same fact. These goals may be in conflict and it is typically not straightforward to find the optimal sweet spot.
In this regard, realistic benchmarks can provide guidance.
While no off-the-shelf standpoint ontologies exist yet, we expect that sensible test cases can be generated from linked open data, ontology alignment settings, or ontology repositories with versioning.

Likewise, the calculus can be analysed and improved in terms of more comprehensive completeness guarantees;
in fact, we conjecture that it already yields all entailed “boxed” assertions and concept inclusions over concept names.

More generally, we will investigate standpoint extensions of other light- or heavyweight ontology languages regarding computational properties and efficient reasoning.

Appendix A
Proofs for
Section

2

Normalisation

We will next show
Lemma

1
.
By introducing new standpoint, concept, and role names, any knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
can be turned into a normalised knowledge base
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
that is a conservative extension of
\mathcal{K}
, i.e., every model of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
is also a model of
\mathcal{K}
, and every model of
\mathcal{K}
can be extended to a model of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
by appropriately choosing the interpretations of the additional standpoint, concept, and role names.

To show that this transformation can be done in polynomial time, yielding a normalised KB
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
whose size is linear in the size of
\mathcal{K}
, we next define the size
\left\lVert\mathcal{K}\right\rVert
of a knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
roughly as the number of symbols needed to write down
\mathcal{K}
, and define it formally as follows.

Definition 2
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge base.
The
size
of
\mathcal{K}
, denoted
\left\lVert\mathcal{K}\right\rVert
, and its various constituents is defined inductively as follows:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathsf{s}_{1}\cap\ldots\cap\mathsf{s}_{n}\preceq\mathsf{s}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}n+1

\displaystyle\text{ for }\mathsf{s}\in N_{\mathsf{S}}\cup\left\{\mathbf{0}\right\}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\top\right\rVert

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}1

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bot\right\rVert

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}1

\displaystyle\left\lVert A\right\rVert

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}1

\displaystyle\text{ for }A\in N_{\mathsf{C}}

\displaystyle\left\lVert C\sqcap D\right\rVert

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}1+\left\lVert C\right\rVert+\left\lVert D\right\rVert

\displaystyle\left\lVert\exists R.C\right\rVert

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}1+\left\lVert C\right\rVert

\displaystyle\left\lVert\exists R.\mathsf{Self}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}1

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathord{\mathop{\odot}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}C\right\rVert

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}1+\left\lVert C\right\rVert

\displaystyle\left\lVert C\sqsubseteq D\right\rVert

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}1+\left\lVert C\right\rVert+\left\lVert D\right\rVert

\displaystyle\left\lVert R_{1}\circ\ldots\circ R_{n}\sqsubseteq R\right\rVert

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}n+1

\displaystyle\left\lVert C(a)\right\rVert

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}1+\left\lVert C\right\rVert

\displaystyle\left\lVert R(a_{1},a_{2})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}3

\displaystyle\left\lVert\neg\xi\right\rVert

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}1+\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert

\displaystyle\left\lVert\lambda_{1}\mathrel{\wedge}\ldots\mathrel{\wedge}\lambda_{n}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathord{\mathop{\odot}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[\mu]\right\rVert

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}1+\left\lVert\mu\right\rVert

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{K}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}\sum_{\psi\in\mathcal{K}}\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert

❑

The subsequent proof is, for the most parts, an extension of our previous proof for a fragment of the language
[
2023
]
where in this work we added the cases for the new constructors and phase 1 normalisation rules.
We reproduce the whole proof here for coherence and reference.

Proof of
Lemma

1
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{E\!L}+}
knowledge base.
The statements in
\mathcal{K}
can be converted into normal form by exhaustively applying the replacement rules shown on page
2
.
In what follows, denote by
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
the result of exhaustive rule application to
\mathcal{K}
.
To prove the lemma, we proceed to show the following:

1.

Polynomial runtime and linear output size: Application of the normalisation rules terminates after at most a polynomial (in the size of
\mathcal{K}
) number of steps, and the size of the resulting KB
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
is at most linear in the size of
\mathcal{K}
.

2.

Syntactic Correctness:
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
is in normal form according to
Definition

1
.

3.

Semantic Correctness:

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
is a conservative extension of
\mathcal{K}
, more specifically:

(a)

For every model
\mathfrak{D}
of
\mathcal{K}
there exists a DL standpoint structure
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
that extends
\mathfrak{D}
(agrees with
\mathfrak{D}
on the vocabulary of
\mathcal{K}
) and that is a model of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
.

(b)

Every model
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
is also a model of
\mathcal{K}
.

1.

We first show that overall normalisation must terminate after at most
\left\lVert\mathcal{K}\right\rVert
normalisation rule applications.
The proof plan is as follows:
For phase 1, we observe that application of a phase 1 rule
(n)
produces statements to which rule
(n)
is not applicable again.
Rule (
18
) incurs a linear blowup in size for the monomial in question (a constant size increase for each literal in the monomial);
all other phase 1 rules induce only a constant size increase per application, so the overall size increase of normalisation phase 1 is linear.

For phase 2, we concentrate on sets of GCIs (as they are the only places where nested concept terms can occur due to rule (
27
)) and denote
\mathcal{K}
by
\mathcal{T}
(for TBox) if only GCIs are involved, and proceed thus:
We define the multiset
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{T}}:\mathsf{SC}_{\mathcal{T}}\to\mathbb{N}
that contains one copy for each occurence of a concept term occurring in
\mathcal{T}
.
We observe that for each complex concept
\bar{C}
causing some GCI not to be in normal form, a constant number of normalisation rule applications can be used to strictly decrease the cardinality of
\bar{C}
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{T}}
.
Together with the fact that
\sum_{D\in\mathsf{SC}_{\mathcal{T}}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{T}}(D)\leq\left\lVert\mathcal{T}\right\rVert
, the claim then follows.

Define the set
\mathsf{SC}_{\mathcal{T}}
as the least set such that:

•

If
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[C\sqsubseteq D]\in\mathcal{T}
, then
C,D\in\mathsf{SC}_{\mathcal{T}}
;

•

if
C\in\mathsf{SC}_{\mathcal{T}}
, then for all subconcepts
C^{\prime}
of
C
(written
C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{sub}(C)
) we have
C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{SC}_{\mathcal{T}}
.

Now for any TBox
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
(typically obtained from
\mathcal{T}
by applying zero or more normalisation rules), the multiset
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\colon\mathsf{SC}_{\mathcal{T}}\to\mathbb{N}
is then as follows:

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[D\sqsubseteq E]\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\left(c(C,D)+c(C,E)\right)

where we define the concept-counting function
c\colon\mathsf{SC}_{\mathcal{T}}\times\mathsf{SC}_{\mathcal{T}}\to\mathbb{N}
inductively:

\displaystyle c(C,D)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\,:=\,}\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }C=D,\\
c(C,E)&\text{ if }C\neq D\text{ and }[D=\exists R.E\text{ or }D=\mathord{\mathop{\odot}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}E]\\
c(C,E_{1})+c(C,E_{2})&\text{ if }C\neq D\text{ and }D=E_{1}\sqcap E_{2}\\
0&\text{ if }C\neq D\text{ and }D\in N_{\mathsf{C}}\cup\left\{\top,\bot\right\}\end{cases}

For example if
D=\top\sqcap\mathord{\mathop{\lozenge}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}(\top\sqcap\exists R.(\top\sqcap\top))
, then
c(\top,D)=4
while
c(\top\sqcap\top,D)=1
.

We next relate the overall cardinality (sum of number of occurrences) of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{T}}
to the size
\left\lVert\mathcal{T}\right\rVert
on the basis that both can be represented as disjoint unions and we can therefore sum up the individual cardinalities.

Claim 1
.

We have
\sum_{C\in\mathsf{SC}_{\mathcal{T}}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{T}}(C)\leq\left\lVert\mathcal{T}\right\rVert
.

Proof of Claim
.

As
\left\lVert\mathcal{T}\right\rVert=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\left\lVert\tau\right\rVert
and

\sum_{C\in\mathsf{SC}_{\mathcal{T}}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{T}}(C)=\sum_{C\in\mathsf{SC}_{\mathcal{T}}}\sum_{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[D\sqsubseteq E]\in\mathcal{T}}\left(c(C,D)+c(C,E)\right)=\sum_{\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[D\sqsubseteq E]\in\mathcal{T}}\sum_{C\in\mathsf{SC}_{\mathcal{T}}}\left(c(C,D)+c(C,E)\right)

it suffices to look at a single
\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[D\sqsubseteq E]\in\mathcal{T}
and show

\sum_{C\in\mathsf{SC}_{\mathcal{T}}}\left(c(C,D)+c(C,E)\right)\leq\left\lVert\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[D\sqsubseteq E]\right\rVert

which by

\sum_{C\in\mathsf{SC}_{\mathcal{T}}}\left(c(C,D)+c(C,E)\right)=\sum_{D^{\prime}\in\mathsf{sub}(D)}c(D^{\prime},D)+\sum_{E^{\prime}\in\mathsf{sub}(E)}c(E^{\prime},E)

develops into

\sum_{D^{\prime}\in\mathsf{sub}(D)}c(D^{\prime},D)+\sum_{E^{\prime}\in\mathsf{sub}(E)}c(E^{\prime},E)\leq\left\lVert\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[D\sqsubseteq E]\right\rVert=1+1+\left\lVert D\right\rVert+\left\lVert E\right\rVert

for which it suffices to show that for any concept
C\in\mathsf{SC}_{\mathcal{T}}
, we have
\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{sub}(C)}c(C^{\prime},C)\leq\left\lVert C\right\rVert
, which we show by induction.

•

The base case is clear, as
\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{sub}(C)}c(C^{\prime},C)=c(C,C)=1\leq 1=\left\lVert C\right\rVert
for any
C\in N_{\mathsf{C}}\cup\left\{\top,\bot\right\}
.

•

C=\exists R.D
:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{sub}(\exists R.D)}c(C^{\prime},\exists R.D)

\displaystyle=c(\exists R.D,\exists R.D)+\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{sub}(D)}c(C^{\prime},D)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(IH)}}}{{\leq}}=c(\exists R.D,\exists R.D)+\left\lVert D\right\rVert

\displaystyle=1+\left\lVert D\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\exists R.D\right\rVert

•

C=\mathord{\mathop{\odot}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}D
: Similar.

•

C=C_{1}\sqcap C_{2}
:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{sub}(C_{1}\sqcap C_{2})}c(C^{\prime},C_{1}\sqcap C_{2})

\displaystyle=c(C_{1}\sqcap C_{2},C_{1}\sqcap C_{2})+\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{sub}(C_{1})}c(C^{\prime},C_{1})+\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{sub}(C_{2})}c(C^{\prime},C_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(IH)}}}{{\leq}}c(C_{1}\sqcap C_{2},C_{1}\sqcap C_{2})+\left\lVert C_{1}\right\rVert+\left\lVert C_{2}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=1+\left\lVert C_{1}\right\rVert+\left\lVert C_{2}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert C_{1}\sqcap C_{2}\right\rVert

This concludes the proof of
1
.

❑

To prove an overall linear number of rule applications, we next show that for each complex concept
\bar{C}
whose occurence in a GCI
\tau\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
causes
\tau
not to be in normal form, there is a constant number of rule applications (that is, constant for all TBoxes) such that after rule application, the number of overall occurrences of
\bar{C}
has strictly decreased and additionally, any intermediate complex concepts introduced by the rule have been normalised in turn.
To this end, we need to define some more notions.
Let
\mathcal{T}
be a TBox and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
be obtained by application of an arbitrary number of phase 2 normalisation rules.
We say that a complex concept
\bar{C}
occurring in a GCI
\tau\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is a
culprit for
\tau
iff
\tau
is not in normal form because of
\bar{C}
;
a culprit
\bar{C}
is
top-level
for
\tau=\mathord{\mathop{\square}\nolimits_{\mathsf{s}}}[D\sqsubseteq E]
iff
\bar{C}=D
or
\bar{C}=E
.
We say that
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is
faithful to
\mathcal{T}
iff every culprit occurring in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
already occurs in
\mathcal{T}
.
In the proof below, we show that although normalisation rules sometimes introduce new culprits, those culprits will not lead to problems because each one can only cause a constant overhead.

Claim 2
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
